========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, uh, thanks for seeing me. I just... I don't know, I guess I finally made the appointment because my brain just won't stop lately. It’s mostly about work. I’m a computer systems analyst, and I’ve been doing it a long time, but recently I just keep... I keep beating myself up over absolutely everything. I sit there at my desk and I just have this overwhelming, heavy feeling that I'm not really senior material. 

Like, I only have my high school diploma, and I worked my way up, but now I'm convinced I just fooled everyone into thinking I could handle way more than I actually can. And it's just exhausting, you know? Just constantly thinking that they're going to figure out I'm some kind of fraud, that I somehow tricked management into giving me these responsibilities. I mean, I'll spend an hour just staring at a basic system configuration or an email I need to send, just agonizing over it, thinking, well, this is the moment they realize I have no business being here. It just plays on a loop in my head all day, this guilt that I'm letting everyone down and faking my way through it, and I just couldn't take listening to myself think about it anymore.

Well, I mean, the thoughts definitely happen over and over, like I was just saying with the whole fraud thing. It just loops and loops. But as for doing something specific to make it go away? No, not really. I mean, I honestly wish I had some sort of trick or habit like that, to be honest. 

Like, you mentioned organizing? My desk is actually a complete disaster right now. You'd think a systems analyst would have everything perfectly filed and, I don't know, color-coded or something. And maybe back when I started, I was a bit more on top of that kind of physical organization, but lately... no. I don't organize anything to feel better. I don't go around checking things or whatever it is people do. I mean, I do stare at my screen, like I said about the emails, but that's not... it's not a ritual or a routine to make the feeling stop, you know? It's just pure hesitation. I'm just frozen. 

I don't tap my desk or count things or try to think a certain happy thought to cancel it out. I mostly just kind of freeze up and let the thoughts wash over me. I just stew in it, really. It’s like I’m trapped in my office chair, just listening to my own brain tell me I’m completely incompetent, and I don't do anything to shut it off. I just take it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I have. Over the last two weeks, maybe even a bit longer to be completely honest, it's just been... well, it's exactly like you said, feeling down. But it's more of this constant, heavy, low feeling. 

Most days lately, I wake up and there's just this immediate weight on my chest. It's like I’m just moving through work and home with the color turned down, if that makes any sense. Like everything is just washed out and gray. I drive to the office, and I'm just staring at the bumper in front of me, not really listening to the radio, not caring about the traffic, just going through the motions. And then I get to my desk, and like I was saying before about staring at the screen and feeling like a fake, it's also just... I don't have the energy to care. It takes so much physical effort just to lift my arms and type on the keyboard or answer a simple question from a coworker. 

And it's definitely not just at the office, either. I get home, and usually, I'd at least try to decompress. I used to really enjoy tinkering with some old hardware I have out in the garage, you know, setting up little home servers and messing with Linux distros just for fun. It's something I've done for years. But I haven't even turned the lights on in the garage for weeks. I just walk in the front door, drop my keys, sit on the couch, and stare at the wall or flip through channels without actually watching a single show. It’s just this pervasive, dull heaviness. I don't feel like doing the things I usually like, I don't feel like talking to anyone, I just feel like I'm dragging a hundred-pound sack of sand around with me everywhere I go, from the moment my alarm goes off until I finally manage to go back to sleep. So, yeah, depressed and down is a pretty accurate way to put it, because it really does just feel like all the brightness and interest has been completely sucked out of my life right now.

I don't go around checking things or whatever it is people do. It's like I’m just moving through work and home with the color turned down, if that makes any sense. Like everything is just washed out and gray. I drive to the office, and I'm just staring at the bumper in front of me, not really listening to the radio, not caring about the traffic, just going through the motions. And then I get to my desk, and like I was saying before about staring at the screen and feeling like a fake, it's also just... I don't have the energy to care. It's something I've done for years. But I haven't even turned the lights on in the garage for weeks. I just walk in the front door, drop my keys, sit on the couch, and stare at the wall or flip through channels without actually watching a single show. It’s just this pervasive, dull heaviness. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hmm, moving or speaking slowly... I mean, I don't think so? Like, I know I just said I feel incredibly heavy, like I'm dragging that sack of sand around, but that's more of a... an internal lack of motivation, I guess. It's not like my actual physical body is operating in slow motion. If a coworker comes up to my cubicle and asks me to check a server log, I turn around and answer them at a normal speed. I don't sound like a record playing at the wrong RPM or anything like that. At least, nobody has pointed it out to me. You're listening to me right now, so you can probably tell, but I feel like I'm talking at my regular pace. And my wife would definitely tell me if I was talking weirdly or moving like a zombie. She notices absolutely everything. 

And as for being restless or fidgety? No, definitely the opposite of that. Like I said, I mostly just freeze. I have this ergonomic chair at work—they ordered it for me a couple of years back because sitting at a terminal for nine, ten hours a day was absolutely killing my lower back, right in the lumbar area, you know how it is—and I just sink into it and stay completely still. I don't bounce my leg, I don't pace around the office, I don't click my pen. 

I actually used to share a workspace with this guy, Greg, who would constantly tap his foot against the metal desk frame, just this endless tapping all day long, and it drove me absolutely up the wall. I always promised myself I'd never be that guy who distracts everyone else with his nervous energy. So no, I don't fidget or pace or need to get up and walk around. I just sit there, completely planted. I guess I'm just... stationary. But yeah, no weird slow-motion stuff and definitely no pacing around. Just sitting and thinking too much.

Over the last two weeks, maybe even a bit longer to be completely honest, it's just been... well, it's exactly like you said, feeling down. But it's more of this constant, heavy, low feeling. \n\nMost days lately, I wake up and there's just this immediate weight on my chest. It's like I’m just moving through work and home with the color turned down, if that makes any sense. Like everything is just washed out and gray. I drive to the office, and I'm just staring at the bumper in front of me, not really listening to the radio, not caring about the traffic, just going through the motions. And then I get to my desk, and like I was saying before about staring at the screen and feeling like a fake, it's also just... I don't have the energy to care. It takes so much physical effort just to lift my arms and type on the keyboard or answer a simple question from a coworker. \n\nAnd it's definitely not just at the office, either. I get home, and usually, I'd at least try to decompress. I used to really enjoy tinkering with some old hardware I have out in the garage, you know, setting up little home servers and messing with Linux distros just for fun. It's something I've done for years. But I haven't even turned the lights on in the garage for weeks. I just walk in the front door, drop my keys, sit on the couch, and stare at the wall or flip through channels without actually watching a single show. It’s just this pervasive, dull heaviness. I don't feel like doing the things I usually like, I don't feel like talking to anyone, I just feel like I'm dragging a hundred-pound sack of sand around with me everywhere I go, from the moment my alarm goes off until I finally manage to go back to sleep. So, yeah, depressed and down is a pretty accurate way to put it, because it really does just feel like all the brightness and interest has been completely sucked out of my life right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the opposite end, have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Well, hmm, moving or speaking slowly... I mean, I don't think so? Like, I know I just said I feel incredibly heavy, like I'm dragging that sack of sand around, but that's more of a... an internal lack of motivation, I guess. It's not like my actual physical body is operating in slow motion. If a coworker comes up to my cubicle and asks me to check a server log, I turn around and answer them at a normal speed. I don't sound like a record playing at the wrong RPM or anything like that. At least, nobody has pointed it out to me. You're listening to me right now, so you can probably tell, but I feel like I'm talking at my regular pace. And my wife would definitely tell me if I was talking weirdly or moving like a zombie. She notices absolutely everything. \n\nAnd as for being restless or fidgety? No, definitely the opposite of that. Like I said, I mostly just freeze. I have this ergonomic chair at work—they ordered it for me a couple of years back because sitting at a terminal for nine, ten hours a day was absolutely killing my lower back, right in the lumbar area, you know how it is—and I just sink into it and stay completely still. I don't bounce my leg, I don't pace around the office, I don't click my pen. \n\nI actually used to share a workspace with this guy, Greg, who would constantly tap his foot against the metal desk frame, just this endless tapping all day long, and it drove me absolutely up the wall. I always promised myself I'd never be that guy who distracts everyone else with his nervous energy. So no, I don't fidget or pace or need to get up and walk around. I just sit there, completely planted. I guess I'm just... stationary. But yeah, no weird slow-motion stuff and definitely no pacing around. Just sitting and thinking too much.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt unusually guilty about things or believed that you are worthless or deserve to be punished?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_systems_analyst / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I keep beating myself up that I’m not really senior material and that I fooled everyone into thinking I could handle more than I can.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, actually... that’s really the main reason I finally made the appointment to come in. I just keep beating myself up, constantly. It ties right back into that whole fraud thing I mentioned earlier. I just have this overwhelming, sinking feeling that I’m not really senior material. 

You know, they gave me that senior systems analyst title a while back, and I don't even have a college degree—just my high school diploma and a lot of years grinding it out, learning on the job. Which was fine for a long time, but lately, I just feel this massive, crushing guilt, like I’ve somehow run this huge con on my boss, the company, everybody. I sit in that ergonomic chair I told you about, and I just think about how I fooled everyone into thinking I could handle way more than I actually can. 

It makes me feel completely worthless. Like, I’m taking up a senior spot, taking the salary, and I can't even muster the energy to do the basic tasks anymore without freezing up. I feel like I'm stealing from them, honestly. And that’s where the punishment part comes in, I guess. I don't know if I think I deserve to be, like, physically punished, but I definitely feel like I deserve to be fired. Stripped of the title, marched out of the building, humiliated... because I brought it on myself by pretending I was smarter or more capable than I really am. 

I look at the other guys on my team who went to university for this stuff, who actually know the theory behind what we're doing, and I just feel so incredibly guilty for even sitting in the same row of cubicles as them. I shouldn't be there. I just beat myself up over it all day long. It's like a loop I can't get out of, just constantly telling myself how useless I am and how I've tricked everyone.

Episode Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: Looking back, the low, guilty periods have been hanging around off and on for about eleven months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_systems_analyst / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless most of the time, or have you lost interest or pleasure in most of your usual activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Over the last two weeks, maybe even a bit longer to be completely honest, it's just been... well, it's exactly like you said, feeling down. But it's more of this constant, heavy, low feeling. \n\nMost days lately, I wake up and there's just this immediate weight on my chest. It's like I’m just moving through work and home with the color turned down, if that makes any sense. Like everything is just washed out and gray. I drive to the office, and I'm just staring at the bumper in front of me, not really listening to the radio, not caring about the traffic, just going through the motions. And then I get to my desk, and like I was saying before about staring at the screen and feeling like a fake, it's also just... I don't have the energy to care. It takes so much physical effort just to lift my arms and type on the keyboard or answer a simple question from a coworker. \n\nAnd it's definitely not just at the office, either. I mean, I don't think so? Like, I know I just said I feel incredibly heavy, like I'm dragging that sack of sand around, but that's more of a... an internal lack of motivation, I guess. It's not like my actual physical body is operating in slow motion. If a coworker comes up to my cubicle and asks me to check a server log, I turn around and answer them at a normal speed. I don't sound like a record playing at the wrong RPM or anything like that. At least, nobody has pointed it out to me. You're listening to me right now, so you can probably tell, but I feel like I'm talking at my regular pace. And my wife would definitely tell me if I was talking weirdly or moving like a zombie. She notices absolutely everything. \n\nAnd as for being restless or fidgety? No, definitely the opposite of that. Like I said, I mostly just freeze. I have this ergonomic chair at work—they ordered it for me a couple of years back because sitting at a terminal for nine, ten hours a day was absolutely killing my lower back, right in the lumbar area, you know how it is—and I just sink into it and stay completely still. I don't bounce my leg, I don't pace around the office, I don't click my pen. \n\nI actually used to share a workspace with this guy, Greg, who would constantly tap his foot against the metal desk frame, just this endless tapping all day long, and it drove me absolutely up the wall. Which was fine for a long time, but lately, I just feel this massive, crushing guilt, like I’ve somehow run this huge con on my boss, the company, everybody. I sit in that ergonomic chair I told you about, and I just think about how I fooled everyone into thinking I could handle way more than I actually can. \n\nIt makes me feel completely worthless. Like, I’m taking up a senior spot, taking the salary, and I can't even muster the energy to do the basic tasks anymore without freezing up. I feel like I'm stealing from them, honestly. Stripped of the title, marched out of the building, humiliated... because I brought it on myself by pretending I was smarter or more capable than I really am. \n\nI look at the other guys on my team who went to university for this stuff, who actually know the theory behind what we're doing, and I just feel so incredibly guilty for even sitting in the same row of cubicles as them. I shouldn't be there. I just beat myself up over it all day long. It's like a loop I can't get out of, just constantly telling myself how useless I am and how I've tricked everyone.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had episodes like this before, where you felt down, guilty, and lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, I wish I could say I just woke up like this a couple of weeks ago and it was a totally new thing, because then maybe it would just be some passing phase, right? Like a bug you catch and just get over. But... no. It's definitely not the first time. 

If I really sit down and trace it back—which is hard, honestly, because the days just kind of blur together into this one long, gray timeline when you feel like this—but looking back, these low, guilty periods have been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now. 

Yeah, almost a full year. I remember specifically because it started right around the time they rolled out this massive system migration at the office. We were moving a bunch of our legacy databases over to a new cloud infrastructure, which was an absolute nightmare logistically, by the way. I thought the stress from that project was just getting to me, you know? Like, anyone would feel overwhelmed trying to map all those old dependencies. So I’d have these weeks back then where I just felt completely drained, totally incompetent, just dragging myself through the days and feeling like I was going to get found out and fired at any second. 

Then maybe the project would hit a milestone and the feeling would lift a little bit, and I'd think, okay, I'm back, I'm fine, it was just the stress of the migration. But then... it would just come back again. It's like this dark cloud that just keeps circling back around. So for the last eleven months, it's just been this exhausting cycle. I'll have a stretch where I'm just barely keeping my head above water, feeling like a total fraud, not even looking at my garage projects, and then maybe a brief window where it's not quite as crushing... but it never really goes away completely. It just keeps coming back. So, yeah, it's been an off-and-on struggle for nearly a year now, not just the last couple of weeks.

So, yeah, it's been an off-and-on struggle for nearly a year now, not just the last couple of weeks.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this past year, have you ever had periods in your life where you felt down, guilty, or lost interest in things for a period of time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_systems_analyst / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Before this past year? Hmm. You mean like, way back before the migration project and all that? Let me think... 

I don't... I don't think so. Not like this. I mean, sure, everyone has bad days, right? Like maybe you mess up a script at work and bring down a server for ten minutes, and you feel like an idiot for the rest of the afternoon. Or maybe you have an argument with your wife about whose turn it was to take the trash out to the curb, and you feel kind of down for the evening. But that's just normal life stuff. It passes. You wake up the next morning, you have your coffee—I always have this dark roast, French press, I need that strong kick to get going—and you're fine. 

But this... this heavy, dragging, worthless feeling, where I just stare at the screen and feel like a total fraud? No, I really don't remember ever feeling this way before the last eleven months or so. I was always pretty steady. I mean, I had to be, right? Working my way up from just a basic helpdesk tech, taking calls from people who didn't know how to plug in a mouse, all the way up to senior systems analyst without a degree... you have to have a certain amount of drive and confidence to do that. I used to be the guy who would jump into a problem, figure out the architecture, map out the solution on the whiteboard. I enjoyed it. I felt capable. 

I never used to sit around beating myself up or feeling guilty for just existing in my cubicle. And my hobbies—like I said, I've got this whole setup in my garage, I was rebuilding this old 1978 pinball machine, a Bally Strikes and Spares, doing all the wiring myself, sourcing original parts off eBay. I loved spending my weekends out there with a beer and a soldering iron. I never lost interest in that stuff before. 

So yeah, if I'm looking back over my whole life, my twenties, my thirties... no. This whole dark cloud thing, this cycle of feeling completely drained and incompetent, it really just started with that system migration about a year ago. Before that, I was just... normal, I guess. Just a regular guy doing his job and fixing up old arcade games. I really don't remember ever dealing with anything like this before.

It takes so much physical effort just to lift my arms and type on the keyboard or answer a simple question from a coworker. \n\nAnd it's definitely not just at the office, either. I get home, and usually, I'd at least try to decompress. I used to really enjoy tinkering with some old hardware I have out in the garage, you know, setting up little home servers and messing with Linux distros just for fun. It's something I've done for years. But I haven't even turned the lights on in the garage for weeks. I just walk in the front door, drop my keys, sit on the couch, and stare at the wall or flip through channels without actually watching a single show. It’s just this pervasive, dull heaviness. I don't feel like doing the things I usually like, I don't feel like talking to anyone, I just feel like I'm dragging a hundred-pound sack of sand around with me everywhere I go, from the moment my alarm goes off until I finally manage to go back to sleep. So, yeah, depressed and down is a pretty accurate way to put it, because it really does just feel like all the brightness and interest has been completely sucked out of my life right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the opposite end, have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Well, hmm, moving or speaking slowly... I mean, I don't think so? Like, I know I just said I feel incredibly heavy, like I'm dragging that sack of sand around, but that's more of a... an internal lack of motivation, I guess. It's not like my actual physical body is operating in slow motion. If a coworker comes up to my cubicle and asks me to check a server log, I turn around and answer them at a normal speed. I don't sound like a record playing at the wrong RPM or anything like that. At least, nobody has pointed it out to me. You're listening to me right now, so you can probably tell, but I feel like I'm talking at my regular pace. And my wife would definitely tell me if I was talking weirdly or moving like a zombie. She notices absolutely everything. \n\nAnd as for being restless or fidgety? No, definitely the opposite of that. Like I said, I mostly just freeze. I have this ergonomic chair at work—they ordered it for me a couple of years back because sitting at a terminal for nine, ten hours a day was absolutely killing my lower back, right in the lumbar area, you know how it is—and I just sink into it and stay completely still. I don't bounce my leg, I don't pace around the office, I don't click my pen. \n\nI actually used to share a workspace with this guy, Greg, who would constantly tap his foot against the metal desk frame, just this endless tapping all day long, and it drove me absolutely up the wall. I always promised myself I'd never be that guy who distracts everyone else with his nervous energy. So no, I don't fidget or pace or need to get up and walk around. I just sit there, completely planted. I guess I'm just... stationary. But yeah, no weird slow-motion stuff and definitely no pacing around. Just sitting and thinking too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt unusually guilty about things or believed that you are worthless or deserve to be punished?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually... that’s really the main reason I finally made the appointment to come in. I just keep beating myself up, constantly. It ties right back into that whole fraud thing I mentioned earlier. I just have this overwhelming, sinking feeling that I’m not really senior material. \n\nYou know, they gave me that senior systems analyst title a while back, and I don't even have a college degree—just my high school diploma and a lot of years grinding it out, learning on the job. Which was fine for a long time, but lately, I just feel this massive, crushing guilt, like I’ve somehow run this huge con on my boss, the company, everybody. I sit in that ergonomic chair I told you about, and I just think about how I fooled everyone into thinking I could handle way more than I actually can. \n\nIt makes me feel completely worthless. Like, I’m taking up a senior spot, taking the salary, and I can't even muster the energy to do the basic tasks anymore without freezing up. I feel like I'm stealing from them, honestly. And that’s where the punishment part comes in, I guess. I don't know if I think I deserve to be, like, physically punished, but I definitely feel like I deserve to be fired. Stripped of the title, marched out of the building, humiliated... because I brought it on myself by pretending I was smarter or more capable than I really am. \n\nI look at the other guys on my team who went to university for this stuff, who actually know the theory behind what we're doing, and I just feel so incredibly guilty for even sitting in the same row of cubicles as them. Two straight weeks? Like, fourteen days in a row of just... feeling like this? No, definitely not. I mean, I really tried to rack my brain just now when you asked the previous question, and honestly, if I had ever felt like this for a solid two weeks before that migration project, I really think I would have remembered it. 

Because, you see, in IT, two weeks is a lifetime. If you're out of it, if you're just staring at a screen and not closing tickets or mapping out the network architecture for two straight weeks, people notice. Your queue backs up. The project managers start pinging you on Teams every five minutes asking for updates. I just couldn't afford to check out like that. I had to be on my game constantly, especially since I didn't have the fancy degree to fall back on. 

And even outside of work, you know, my wife would have definitely noticed if I didn't go out to the garage for two weeks. Like I was saying about that Bally Strikes and Spares machine—when you're restoring something like that, you get a rhythm going. You order a specific solenoid or a flipper rebuild kit, it arrives in the mail, and you're out there that very weekend installing it because you're just so excited to see it work. If I had just let those parts sit in the shipping box on the workbench for two or three weeks because I was too down to care... well, that just never happened back then. I was always tinkering. I was always doing *something*. 

So, yeah, to answer your question, no. I never had a two-week stretch of feeling worthless or losing interest in my projects before this past year. It's like a switch flipped when that whole cloud migration nightmare happened, and before that... I was just my regular self, day in and day out. Just working hard, coming home, messing with the pinball machine, normal stuff. I never had anything that dragged on for weeks on end like this before.

It just plays on a loop in my head all day, this guilt that I'm letting everyone down and faking my way through it, and I just couldn't take listening to myself think about it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having the same upsetting thoughts over and over, and do you feel like you have to do something specific—like checking things, organizing, or thinking certain thoughts—to try to make those feelings go away?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, the thoughts definitely happen over and over, like I was just saying with the whole fraud thing. It just loops and loops. But as for doing something specific to make it go away? No, not really. It's something I've done for years. But I haven't even turned the lights on in the garage for weeks. I just walk in the front door, drop my keys, sit on the couch, and stare at the wall or flip through channels without actually watching a single show. It’s just this pervasive, dull heaviness. I don't feel like doing the things I usually like, I don't feel like talking to anyone, I just feel like I'm dragging a hundred-pound sack of sand around with me everywhere I go, from the moment my alarm goes off until I finally manage to go back to sleep. So, yeah, depressed and down is a pretty accurate way to put it, because it really does just feel like all the brightness and interest has been completely sucked out of my life right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the opposite end, have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Well, hmm, moving or speaking slowly... I mean, I don't think so? Like, I know I just said I feel incredibly heavy, like I'm dragging that sack of sand around, but that's more of a... an internal lack of motivation, I guess. It's not like my actual physical body is operating in slow motion. If a coworker comes up to my cubicle and asks me to check a server log, I turn around and answer them at a normal speed. I don't sound like a record playing at the wrong RPM or anything like that. At least, nobody has pointed it out to me. You're listening to me right now, so you can probably tell, but I feel like I'm talking at my regular pace. And my wife would definitely tell me if I was talking weirdly or moving like a zombie. She notices absolutely everything. \n\nAnd as for being restless or fidgety? No, definitely the opposite of that. Like I said, I mostly just freeze. I have this ergonomic chair at work—they ordered it for me a couple of years back because sitting at a terminal for nine, ten hours a day was absolutely killing my lower back, right in the lumbar area, you know how it is—and I just sink into it and stay completely still. I don't bounce my leg, I don't pace around the office, I don't click my pen. \n\nI actually used to share a workspace with this guy, Greg, who would constantly tap his foot against the metal desk frame, just this endless tapping all day long, and it drove me absolutely up the wall. I always promised myself I'd never be that guy who distracts everyone else with his nervous energy. So no, I don't fidget or pace or need to get up and walk around. I just sit there, completely planted. I guess I'm just... stationary. But yeah, no weird slow-motion stuff and definitely no pacing around. Just sitting and thinking too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt unusually guilty about things or believed that you are worthless or deserve to be punished?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually... that’s really the main reason I finally made the appointment to come in. I just keep beating myself up, constantly. It ties right back into that whole fraud thing I mentioned earlier. I just have this overwhelming, sinking feeling that I’m not really senior material. \n\nYou know, they gave me that senior systems analyst title a while back, and I don't even have a college degree—just my high school diploma and a lot of years grinding it out, learning on the job. Which was fine for a long time, but lately, I just feel this massive, crushing guilt, like I’ve somehow run this huge con on my boss, the company, everybody. I sit in that ergonomic chair I told you about, and I just think about how I fooled everyone into thinking I could handle way more than I actually can. \n\nIt makes me feel completely worthless. Like, I’m taking up a senior spot, taking the salary, and I can't even muster the energy to do the basic tasks anymore without freezing up. I feel like I'm stealing from them, honestly. And that’s where the punishment part comes in, I guess. I don't know if I think I deserve to be, like, physically punished, but I definitely feel like I deserve to be fired. Stripped of the title, marched out of the building, humiliated... because I brought it on myself by pretending I was smarter or more capable than I really am. \n\nI look at the other guys on my team who went to university for this stuff, who actually know the theory behind what we're doing, and I just feel so incredibly guilty for even sitting in the same row of cubicles as them. I shouldn't be there. I just beat myself up over it all day long. It's like a loop I can't get out of, just constantly telling myself how useless I am and how I've tricked everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt down, guilty, and lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I mean, I wish I could say I just woke up like this a couple of weeks ago and it was a totally new thing, because then maybe it would just be some passing phase, right? Like a bug you catch and just get over. But... no. It's definitely not the first time. \n\nIf I really sit down and trace it back—which is hard, honestly, because the days just kind of blur together into this one long, gray timeline when you feel like this—but looking back, these low, guilty periods have been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now. \n\nYeah, almost a full year. I remember specifically because it started right around the time they rolled out this massive system migration at the office. We were moving a bunch of our legacy databases over to a new cloud infrastructure, which was an absolute nightmare logistically, by the way. I thought the stress from that project was just getting to me, you know? Like, anyone would feel overwhelmed trying to map all those old dependencies. So I’d have these weeks back then where I just felt completely drained, totally incompetent, just dragging myself through the days and feeling like I was going to get found out and fired at any second. \n\nThen maybe the project would hit a milestone and the feeling would lift a little bit, and I'd think, okay, I'm back, I'm fine, it was just the stress of the migration. But then... it would just come back again. It's like this dark cloud that just keeps circling back around. So for the last eleven months, it's just been this exhausting cycle. I'll have a stretch where I'm just barely keeping my head above water, feeling like a total fraud, not even looking at my garage projects, and then maybe a brief window where it's not quite as crushing... but it never really goes away completely. It just keeps coming back. So, yeah, it's been an off-and-on struggle for nearly a year now, not just the last couple of weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, have you ever had periods in your life where you felt down, guilty, or lost interest in things for a period of time?\n\nPatient: Before this past year? Hmm. You mean like, way back before the migration project and all that? Let me think... \n\nI don't... I don't think so. Not like this. I mean, sure, everyone has bad days, right? Like maybe you mess up a script at work and bring down a server for ten minutes, and you feel like an idiot for the rest of the afternoon. Or maybe you have an argument with your wife about whose turn it was to take the trash out to the curb, and you feel kind of down for the evening. But that's just normal life stuff. It passes. You wake up the next morning, you have your coffee—I always have this dark roast, French press, I need that strong kick to get going—and you're fine. \n\nBut this... this heavy, dragging, worthless feeling, where I just stare at the screen and feel like a total fraud? No, I really don't remember ever feeling this way before the last eleven months or so. I was always pretty steady. I mean, I had to be, right? Working my way up from just a basic helpdesk tech, taking calls from people who didn't know how to plug in a mouse, all the way up to senior systems analyst without a degree... you have to have a certain amount of drive and confidence to do that. I used to be the guy who would jump into a problem, figure out the architecture, map out the solution on the whiteboard. I enjoyed it. I felt capable. \n\nI never used to sit around beating myself up or feeling guilty for just existing in my cubicle. And my hobbies—like I said, I've got this whole setup in my garage, I was rebuilding this old 1978 pinball machine, a Bally Strikes and Spares, doing all the wiring myself, sourcing original parts off eBay. I loved spending my weekends out there with a beer and a soldering iron. I never lost interest in that stuff before. \n\nSo yeah, if I'm looking back over my whole life, my twenties, my thirties... no. This whole dark cloud thing, this cycle of feeling completely drained and incompetent, it really just started with that system migration about a year ago. Before that, I was just... normal, I guess. Just a regular guy doing his job and fixing up old arcade games. I really don't remember ever dealing with anything like this before.\n\nDoctor: Before this most recent low period that started around the system migration, did you ever have a time when you felt down nearly every day, lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or had other symptoms that lasted for at least two weeks and were different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Two straight weeks? Like, fourteen days in a row of just... feeling like this? No, definitely not. I mean, I really tried to rack my brain just now when you asked the previous question, and honestly, if I had ever felt like this for a solid two weeks before that migration project, I really think I would have remembered it. \n\nBecause, you see, in IT, two weeks is a lifetime. If you're out of it, if you're just staring at a screen and not closing tickets or mapping out the network architecture for two straight weeks, people notice. Your queue backs up. The project managers start pinging you on Teams every five minutes asking for updates. I just couldn't afford to check out like that. I had to be on my game constantly, especially since I didn't have the fancy degree to fall back on. \n\nAnd even outside of work, you know, my wife would have definitely noticed if I didn't go out to the garage for two weeks. Like I was saying about that Bally Strikes and Spares machine—when you're restoring something like that, you get a rhythm going. You order a specific solenoid or a flipper rebuild kit, it arrives in the mail, and you're out there that very weekend installing it because you're just so excited to see it work. If I had just let those parts sit in the shipping box on the workbench for two or three weeks because I was too down to care... well, that just never happened back then. I was always tinkering. I was always doing *something*. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, no. I never had a two-week stretch of feeling worthless or losing interest in my projects before this past year. It's like a switch flipped when that whole cloud migration nightmare happened, and before that... I was just my regular self, day in and day out. Just working hard, coming home, messing with the pinball machine, normal stuff. I never had anything that dragged on for weeks on end like this before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Let me sort of map out the timeline in my head here. So, the initial server migration—the one that really kicked this whole nightmare off—that was about eleven months ago. And since then... well, yeah, I'd say it hasn't just been one continuous, unbroken flatline the entire time. It comes in waves, I guess you could say.

Like, I'll have a stretch—maybe three or four weeks—where I can barely drag myself out of bed, where I sit at my desk staring at the blinking cursor on the command line, just feeling like an absolute fraud. I get so wrapped up in feeling guilty that I'm taking a senior analyst's paycheck when I'm barely closing any tier-two tickets, you know? And I won't even look at the garage during those weeks. 

But then... maybe the project moves into a holding pattern, or we get a breather waiting on vendor support, and I'll have a month or two where that heavy gray fog lifts just enough. I wouldn't say I'm completely back to my old self, but I'm functional again. I can get through the day without beating myself up. I might even go out to the workbench and sort some fuses or clean the playfield glass on the pinball machine. 

But then, sure enough, a couple of months go by and BAM. We have to push a new security patch across the network, or even if nothing at work triggers it, I just wake up one Tuesday and I'm right back down in that hole for another three weeks, feeling completely worthless and drained all over again. So, yeah, to answer your question, it's definitely been more than one distinct time. It’s been this frustrating off-and-on cycle of these heavy periods coming, staying for a few weeks, fading out for a bit, and then coming right back, basically for the last eleven months straight.

I was always doing *something*. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, no. I never had a two-week stretch of feeling worthless or losing interest in my projects before this past year. Just working hard, coming home, messing with the pinball machine, normal stuff. Just going through the motions, completely drained. But before all this started eleven months ago? Never. My life was in full color back then. I was engaged, I was active, I cared about what I was doing. So, no, definitely no two-week stretches of this heavy feeling before this recent stuff. It's just a lately thing.\n\nDoctor: In the past year, have you had more than one distinct period where you felt down, lost interest in things, or felt guilty, where each period lasted for at least two weeks and was separated by at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: Let me sort of map out the timeline in my head here. So, the initial server migration—the one that really kicked this whole nightmare off—that was about eleven months ago. Like, I'll have a stretch—maybe three or four weeks—where I can barely drag myself out of bed, where I sit at my desk staring at the blinking cursor on the command line, just feeling like an absolute fraud. I get so wrapped up in feeling guilty that I'm taking a senior analyst's paycheck when I'm barely closing any tier-two tickets, you know? I wouldn't say I'm completely back to my old self, but I'm functional again. I can get through the day without beating myself up. I might even go out to the workbench and sort some fuses or clean the playfield glass on the pinball machine. \n\nBut then, sure enough, a couple of months go by and BAM. We have to push a new security patch across the network, or even if nothing at work triggers it, I just wake up one Tuesday and I'm right back down in that hole for another three weeks, feeling completely worthless and drained all over again. So, yeah, to answer your question, it's definitely been more than one distinct time. It’s been this frustrating off-and-on cycle of these heavy periods coming, staying for a few weeks, fading out for a bit, and then coming right back, basically for the last eleven months straight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, again, Doc, if you're asking about my whole entire life, going all the way back? Then it's really just what I've been telling you about this past year. If you mean *before* this past year, then absolutely not. 

I know we keep kind of circling back to this, and I don't mean to be difficult or anything, but I really want to be clear that this is a totally new thing for me. Like, my whole life, going back to when I was eighteen, fresh out of high school and just starting at the very bottom of the IT ladder... I never, ever had periods where I just shut down or lost interest in things. I was a grinder. I had to be, you know? 

If I got stressed out back in my twenties or thirties, or if a deployment went sideways, I didn't get depressed for weeks on end. I might get ticked off, sure, or I'd go out to the garage and spend an entire weekend taking apart a flipper assembly on a pinball machine just to burn off the frustration, but I never got that heavy, gray, worthless feeling. Not once. I always bounced right back.

So, if you're asking if I've had multiple periods like this over the course of my *entire* lifetime... well, yes, but *only* if we're just looking at the past eleven months. Those off-and-on waves I just told you about, where I'm down for three weeks and then okay for a month or two? That all fits into this one single timeframe since the server migration started. But before I turned 42? Never. Not a single time. It's like my brain just worked completely fine, completely normal, for four decades, and then this one massive cloud project just broke the camel's back. So, no, no history of this kind of thing at all before this recent stuff.

I was always doing *something*. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, no. I never had a two-week stretch of feeling worthless or losing interest in my projects before this past year. It's like a switch flipped when that whole cloud migration nightmare happened, and before that... I was just my regular self, day in and day out. Just working hard, coming home, messing with the pinball machine, normal stuff. I never had anything that dragged on for weeks on end like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time before this past year when you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more in a row?\n\nPatient: I mean... I know you probably have to ask these things a certain way to check all your boxes on your clipboard or whatever, but I promise you, I've racked my brain and there was nothing like this before that migration project. \n\nIt's really just been this past year, and honestly, most days lately I’ve had this heavy, low feeling, like I’m just moving through work and home with the color turned down. It's the best way I can describe it. You know when you go into the display settings on a monitor and you accidentally drag the saturation slider all the way to the left? Everything just goes completely gray and flat. That's exactly what my life feels like right now. It's just grayscale. \n\nI log into the network in the morning, I check the server logs, I sit in my cubicle, I drive home, I walk right past my workbench and the pinball machine in the garage without even bothering to flip on the overhead lights... and it's all just so gray and heavy. Just going through the motions, completely drained. But before all this started eleven months ago? Never. My life was in full color back then. I was engaged, I was active, I cared about what I was doing. So, no, definitely no two-week stretches of this heavy feeling before this recent stuff. It's just a lately thing.\n\nDoctor: In the past year, have you had more than one distinct period where you felt down, lost interest in things, or felt guilty, where each period lasted for at least two weeks and was separated by at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: Let me sort of map out the timeline in my head here. I never, ever had periods where I just shut down or lost interest in things. I was a grinder. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Two solid months? Like, sixty days in a row of just... nothing but that heavy gray feeling every single day? I'd have to say no to that. I know we're parsing the timeline pretty closely here with all these questions, but let me think about how to explain it... because looking back, the low, guilty periods have been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now, ever since that migration project started. But a solid two-month block? No, I don't think it's ever been two full months without at least some kind of break. 

It’s more like... well, think about how an IT project rollout goes. You have a sprint, right? You're heads down, everything is on fire for three, maybe four weeks, and then you hit a milestone and things stabilize for a minute before the next phase. That's exactly how this has been. I'll get hit with that drained, completely worthless feeling, where I'm just staring at my command line feeling guilty for even cashing my paycheck when I'm so unproductive, and that'll drag on for maybe three weeks. Four weeks tops. 

But then, before it ever hits that two-month mark you're asking about, it always seems to lift just a little bit. Not completely, like I'm not doing cartwheels or anything, but the fog thins out enough that I can breathe for a month or two. I can go out to the garage and at least wipe down the playfield glass on my machines without feeling completely dead inside. 

So, yeah, it's been this off-and-on cycle for eleven months, but never two full months straight of just the absolute bottom. It's always broken up. Like, if I look at my calendar from last fall, when we were migrating the legacy databases, I was really down in October, but by Thanksgiving I was doing okay again, and then... well, you get the idea. It just keeps cycling like that, off and on, for the last eleven months. But never two straight months at a time.

We were moving a bunch of our legacy databases over to a new cloud infrastructure, which was an absolute nightmare logistically, by the way. So I’d have these weeks back then where I just felt completely drained, totally incompetent, just dragging myself through the days and feeling like I was going to get found out and fired at any second. \n\nThen maybe the project would hit a milestone and the feeling would lift a little bit, and I'd think, okay, I'm back, I'm fine, it was just the stress of the migration. But then... it would just come back again. It's like this dark cloud that just keeps circling back around. So for the last eleven months, it's just been this exhausting cycle. I'll have a stretch where I'm just barely keeping my head above water, feeling like a total fraud, not even looking at my garage projects, and then maybe a brief window where it's not quite as crushing... but it never really goes away completely. It just keeps coming back. So, yeah, it's been an off-and-on struggle for nearly a year now, not just the last couple of weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, have you ever had periods in your life where you felt down, guilty, or lost interest in things for a period of time?\n\nPatient: Before this past year? Hmm. You mean like, way back before the migration project and all that? Let me think... \n\nI don't... I don't think so. Not like this. I mean, sure, everyone has bad days, right? Like maybe you mess up a script at work and bring down a server for ten minutes, and you feel like an idiot for the rest of the afternoon. Or maybe you have an argument with your wife about whose turn it was to take the trash out to the curb, and you feel kind of down for the evening. But that's just normal life stuff. It passes. You wake up the next morning, you have your coffee—I always have this dark roast, French press, I need that strong kick to get going—and you're fine. \n\nBut this... this heavy, dragging, worthless feeling, where I just stare at the screen and feel like a total fraud? No, I really don't remember ever feeling this way before the last eleven months or so. I was always pretty steady. I mean, I had to be, right? Working my way up from just a basic helpdesk tech, taking calls from people who didn't know how to plug in a mouse, all the way up to senior systems analyst without a degree... you have to have a certain amount of drive and confidence to do that. I used to be the guy who would jump into a problem, figure out the architecture, map out the solution on the whiteboard. I enjoyed it. I felt capable. \n\nI never used to sit around beating myself up or feeling guilty for just existing in my cubicle. And my hobbies—like I said, I've got this whole setup in my garage, I was rebuilding this old 1978 pinball machine, a Bally Strikes and Spares, doing all the wiring myself, sourcing original parts off eBay. I loved spending my weekends out there with a beer and a soldering iron. I never lost interest in that stuff before. \n\nSo yeah, if I'm looking back over my whole life, my twenties, my thirties... no. This whole dark cloud thing, this cycle of feeling completely drained and incompetent, it really just started with that system migration about a year ago. Before that, I was just... normal, I guess. Just a regular guy doing his job and fixing up old arcade games. Like, fourteen days in a row of just... feeling like this? No, definitely not. If you're out of it, if you're just staring at a screen and not closing tickets or mapping out the network architecture for two straight weeks, people notice. Your queue backs up. I had to be on my game constantly, especially since I didn't have the fancy degree to fall back on. \n\nAnd even outside of work, you know, my wife would have definitely noticed if I didn't go out to the garage for two weeks. If I had just let those parts sit in the shipping box on the workbench for two or three weeks because I was too down to care... well, that just never happened back then. I was always tinkering. I was always doing *something*. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, no. I never had a two-week stretch of feeling worthless or losing interest in my projects before this past year. Just working hard, coming home, messing with the pinball machine, normal stuff. It's the best way I can describe it. You know when you go into the display settings on a monitor and you accidentally drag the saturation slider all the way to the left? Just going through the motions, completely drained. But before all this started eleven months ago? Never. My life was in full color back then. I was engaged, I was active, I cared about what I was doing. So, no, definitely no two-week stretches of this heavy feeling before this recent stuff. It's just a lately thing.\n\nDoctor: In the past year, have you had more than one distinct period where you felt down, lost interest in things, or felt guilty, where each period lasted for at least two weeks and was separated by at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: Let me sort of map out the timeline in my head here. So, yeah, to answer your question, it's definitely been more than one distinct time. I wouldn't say I'm completely back to my old self, but I'm functional again. I can get through the day without beating myself up. I might even go out to the workbench and sort some fuses or clean the playfield glass on the pinball machine. \n\nBut then, sure enough, a couple of months go by and BAM. We have to push a new security patch across the network, or even if nothing at work triggers it, I just wake up one Tuesday and I'm right back down in that hole for another three weeks, feeling completely worthless and drained all over again. So, yeah, to answer your question, it's definitely been more than one distinct time. It’s been this frustrating off-and-on cycle of these heavy periods coming, staying for a few weeks, fading out for a bit, and then coming right back, basically for the last eleven months straight.\n\nDoctor: In your lifetime, have you ever had two or more separate periods where you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more, with at least a couple of months in between them?\n\nPatient: I mean, again, Doc, if you're asking about my whole entire life, going all the way back? I sit in that ergonomic chair I told you about, and I just think about how I fooled everyone into thinking I could handle way more than I actually can. \n\nIt makes me feel completely worthless. Like, I’m taking up a senior spot, taking the salary, and I can't even muster the energy to do the basic tasks anymore without freezing up. I feel like I'm stealing from them, honestly. And that’s where the punishment part comes in, I guess. I don't know if I think I deserve to be, like, physically punished, but I definitely feel like I deserve to be fired. Stripped of the title, marched out of the building, humiliated... because I brought it on myself by pretending I was smarter or more capable than I really am. \n\nI look at the other guys on my team who went to university for this stuff, who actually know the theory behind what we're doing, and I just feel so incredibly guilty for even sitting in the same row of cubicles as them. I shouldn't be there. I just beat myself up over it all day long. It's like a loop I can't get out of, just constantly telling myself how useless I am and how I've tricked everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt down, guilty, and lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I mean, I wish I could say I just woke up like this a couple of weeks ago and it was a totally new thing, because then maybe it would just be some passing phase, right? Like a bug you catch and just get over. But... no. It's definitely not the first time. \n\nIf I really sit down and trace it back—which is hard, honestly, because the days just kind of blur together into this one long, gray timeline when you feel like this—but looking back, these low, guilty periods have been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now. \n\nYeah, almost a full year. I remember specifically because it started right around the time they rolled out this massive system migration at the office. We were moving a bunch of our legacy databases over to a new cloud infrastructure, which was an absolute nightmare logistically, by the way. I thought the stress from that project was just getting to me, you know? Like, anyone would feel overwhelmed trying to map all those old dependencies. So I’d have these weeks back then where I just felt completely drained, totally incompetent, just dragging myself through the days and feeling like I was going to get found out and fired at any second. \n\nThen maybe the project would hit a milestone and the feeling would lift a little bit, and I'd think, okay, I'm back, I'm fine, it was just the stress of the migration. But then... it would just come back again. It's like this dark cloud that just keeps circling back around. So for the last eleven months, it's just been this exhausting cycle. I'll have a stretch where I'm just barely keeping my head above water, feeling like a total fraud, not even looking at my garage projects, and then maybe a brief window where it's not quite as crushing... but it never really goes away completely. It just keeps coming back. So, yeah, it's been an off-and-on struggle for nearly a year now, not just the last couple of weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, have you ever had periods in your life where you felt down, guilty, or lost interest in things for a period of time?\n\nPatient: Before this past year? Hmm. You mean like, way back before the migration project and all that? Let me think... \n\nI don't... I don't think so. Not like this. I mean, sure, everyone has bad days, right? Like maybe you mess up a script at work and bring down a server for ten minutes, and you feel like an idiot for the rest of the afternoon. Or maybe you have an argument with your wife about whose turn it was to take the trash out to the curb, and you feel kind of down for the evening. But that's just normal life stuff. It passes. You wake up the next morning, you have your coffee—I always have this dark roast, French press, I need that strong kick to get going—and you're fine. \n\nBut this... this heavy, dragging, worthless feeling, where I just stare at the screen and feel like a total fraud? No, I really don't remember ever feeling this way before the last eleven months or so. I was always pretty steady. I mean, I had to be, right? Working my way up from just a basic helpdesk tech, taking calls from people who didn't know how to plug in a mouse, all the way up to senior systems analyst without a degree... you have to have a certain amount of drive and confidence to do that. I used to be the guy who would jump into a problem, figure out the architecture, map out the solution on the whiteboard. I enjoyed it. I felt capable. \n\nI never used to sit around beating myself up or feeling guilty for just existing in my cubicle. And my hobbies—like I said, I've got this whole setup in my garage, I was rebuilding this old 1978 pinball machine, a Bally Strikes and Spares, doing all the wiring myself, sourcing original parts off eBay. I loved spending my weekends out there with a beer and a soldering iron. I never lost interest in that stuff before. \n\nSo yeah, if I'm looking back over my whole life, my twenties, my thirties... no. This whole dark cloud thing, this cycle of feeling completely drained and incompetent, it really just started with that system migration about a year ago. Before that, I was just... normal, I guess. Just a regular guy doing his job and fixing up old arcade games. I really don't remember ever dealing with anything like this before.\n\nDoctor: Before this most recent low period that started around the system migration, did you ever have a time when you felt down nearly every day, lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or had other symptoms that lasted for at least two weeks and were different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Two straight weeks? Like, fourteen days in a row of just... feeling like this? No, definitely not. I mean, I really tried to rack my brain just now when you asked the previous question, and honestly, if I had ever felt like this for a solid two weeks before that migration project, I really think I would have remembered it. \n\nBecause, you see, in IT, two weeks is a lifetime. If you're out of it, if you're just staring at a screen and not closing tickets or mapping out the network architecture for two straight weeks, people notice. Your queue backs up. The project managers start pinging you on Teams every five minutes asking for updates. I just couldn't afford to check out like that. I had to be on my game constantly, especially since I didn't have the fancy degree to fall back on. \n\nAnd even outside of work, you know, my wife would have definitely noticed if I didn't go out to the garage for two weeks. You order a specific solenoid or a flipper rebuild kit, it arrives in the mail, and you're out there that very weekend installing it because you're just so excited to see it work. If I had just let those parts sit in the shipping box on the workbench for two or three weeks because I was too down to care... well, that just never happened back then. I was always tinkering. I was always doing *something*. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, no. I never had a two-week stretch of feeling worthless or losing interest in my projects before this past year. It's like a switch flipped when that whole cloud migration nightmare happened, and before that... I was just my regular self, day in and day out. Just working hard, coming home, messing with the pinball machine, normal stuff. I never had anything that dragged on for weeks on end like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time before this past year when you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more in a row?\n\nPatient: I mean... I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, Doc, but no. Truly, no. Like I was just saying, before this past year, I never had a two-week period where I felt like this. I know you probably have to ask these things a certain way to check all your boxes on your clipboard or whatever, but I promise you, I've racked my brain and there was nothing like this before that migration project. \n\nIt's really just been this past year, and honestly, most days lately I’ve had this heavy, low feeling, like I’m just moving through work and home with the color turned down. It's the best way I can describe it. You know when you go into the display settings on a monitor and you accidentally drag the saturation slider all the way to the left? Everything just goes completely gray and flat. That's exactly what my life feels like right now. It's just grayscale. \n\nI log into the network in the morning, I check the server logs, I sit in my cubicle, I drive home, I walk right past my workbench and the pinball machine in the garage without even bothering to flip on the overhead lights... and it's all just so gray and heavy. Just going through the motions, completely drained. But before all this started eleven months ago? Never. My life was in full color back then. I was engaged, I was active, I cared about what I was doing. So, no, definitely no two-week stretches of this heavy feeling before this recent stuff. It's just a lately thing.\n\nDoctor: In the past year, have you had more than one distinct period where you felt down, lost interest in things, or felt guilty, where each period lasted for at least two weeks and was separated by at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: Let me sort of map out the timeline in my head here. I mean, I get it, in IT troubleshooting, sometimes you have to verify the baseline twice just to make absolutely sure you're not chasing a ghost in the machine, but... no. Before the past year? Absolutely not. 

Like I just told you, looking back, the low, guilty periods have been hanging around off and on for about eleven months. That is the entire scope of the outage, so to speak. Just those eleven months. Before that? I was completely fine. I mean, I've been working in systems analysis since I was fresh out of high school, pulling all-nighters, dealing with catastrophic server failures, user errors that would make you want to pull your hair out, and I never, ever got dragged down into this kind of funk. I'd just drink my coffee, fix the routing tables, and go home to rebuild the flippers on my pinball machines. I was always the reliable guy. 

It wasn't until this specific massive migration project kicked off that things went sideways for me. So, to be 100% crystal clear for your checklist so we can move on to the next section: before this eleven-month window of off-and-on misery... no. Zero times. Not three times, not one time.
